In papain, the catalytic site contains the sequence &quot;CGSCW&quot; where the cysteine serves as the nucleophile that attacks the peptide bond of the substrate. The pattern &quot;GCTS&quot; in our sequence corresponds to the position of the catalytic cysteine in these enzymes.<br><br>This is distinct from:<br>- **Serine proteases**, which typically feature a &quot;GDSGG&quot; motif with the serine as nucleophile — no such motif is present here.<br>- **Histidine and Aspartate** residues, which are typically part of catalytic triads (serving as general base or stabilizing roles) but are NOT the nucleophile themselves in most enzyme classes.<br><br>The combination of the signal peptide, the &quot;GLGCTS&quot; motif, and the overall sequence architecture strongly identifies this as a papain-family cysteine protease, where **Cysteine** is the active-site nucleophile that performs nucleophilic attack on the substrate&#x27;s carbonyl carbon.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> When evaluating this protein’s hydrolytic mechanism, which amino-acid side chain is the catalytic nucleophile, rather than merely a chemically plausible participant?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Cysteine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Serine<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Histidine<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Aspartate<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0000:2<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Analyzing the protein sequence, I first note the N-terminal signal peptide (MLLLQIILLLLPVICSAGD), indicating a secreted protein. Looking for functional motifs, I identify &quot;GLGCTSIVA&quot; which is a variant of the classic GXSXG nucleophilic elbow motif found in serine hydrolases (particularly patatin-like phospholipases and other α/β-hydrolase fold enzymes).<br><br>The catalytic mechanism of serine hydrolases involves a catalytic triad (Ser-His-Asp/Glu), but the question specifically asks about the **catalytic nucleophile** — the residue that directly attacks the substrate&#x27;s electrophilic center.<br><br>Key distinctions among the choices:<br>- **Serine (B)**: Acts as the **nucleophile** — its hydroxyl group attacks the carbonyl carbon of the substrate, forming a covalent acyl-enzyme intermediate. This is the defining nucleophilic step.<br>- **Histidine (C)**: Acts as a **general base** — it deprotonates the serine hydroxyl to enhance its nucleophilicity, but it does NOT attack the substrate itself.<br>- **Aspartate (D)**: **Stabilizes** the histidine via hydrogen bonding/orientation; it is neither a nucleophile nor directly involved in bond formation/breakage with the substrate.<br>- **Cysteine (A)**: While cysteine can be a nucleophile in cysteine proteases (like papain), this protein&#x27;s motif (GxSxG variant) and overall architecture indicate a serine hydrolase, not a cysteine enzyme.<br><br>The question&#x27;s phrasing (&quot;rather than merely a chemically plausible participant&quot;) emphasizes that while His and Asp participate in the mechanism, only Serine performs the actual nucleophilic attack.<br><br>2. answer: B</td>
</tr></tbody>

In serine hydrolases, the catalytic nucleophile is the serine residue. While histidine and aspartate are part of the catalytic triad (acting as general base and stabilizing residue respectively), the actual nucleophile that attacks the substrate is the serine.

The question asks specifically about the &quot;catalytic nucleophile, rather than merely a chemically plausible participant.&quot; This is a key distinction:

- Histidine: Acts as a general base (proton shuttle), not a nucleophile
- Aspartate: Stabilizes the histidine, not a nucleophile

So even though all three could be &quot;chemically plausible participants&quot; in the reaction mechanism, only serine acts as the nucleophile.
